### ScreenCoder: Advancing Visual-to-Code Generation for Front-End Automation via Modular Multimodal Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.22827v1)
- **Authors**: Yilei Jiang, Yaozhi Zheng, Yuxuan Wan, Jiaming Han, Qunzhong Wang, Michael R. Lyu, Xiangyu Yue
- **Abstract**: Automating the transformation of user interface (UI) designs into front-end code holds significant promise for accelerating software development and democratizing design workflows. While recent large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated progress in text-to-code generation, many existing approaches rely solely on natural language prompts, limiting their effectiveness in capturing spatial layout and visual design intent. In contrast, UI development in practice is inherently multimodal, often starting from visual sketches or mockups. To address this gap, we introduce a modular multi-agent framework that performs UI-to-code generation in three interpretable stages: grounding, planning, and generation. The grounding agent uses a vision-language model to detect and label UI components, the planning agent constructs a hierarchical layout using front-end engineering priors, and the generation agent produces HTML/CSS code via adaptive prompt-based synthesis. This design improves robustness, interpretability, and fidelity over end-to-end black-box methods. Furthermore, we extend the framework into a scalable data engine that automatically produces large-scale image-code pairs. Using these synthetic examples, we fine-tune and reinforce an open-source VLM, yielding notable gains in UI understanding and code quality. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance in layout accuracy, structural coherence, and code correctness. Our code is made publicly available at https://github.com/leigest519/ScreenCoder.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ScreenCoder, a modular multi-agent framework designed to improve UI-to-code generation. Addressing limitations in existing approaches that primarily rely on natural language prompts, ScreenCoder leverages visual inputs (UI screenshots or sketches) to generate front-end code (HTML/CSS).  The framework decomposes the task into three stages: grounding (using a vision-language model to detect and label UI components), planning (constructing a hierarchical layout using front-end engineering knowledge), and generation (producing code through adaptive prompt-based synthesis). Furthermore, the authors create a data engine based on the framework to generate synthetic image-code pairs, which are then used to fine-tune an open-source VLM (Qwen2.5-VL), improving its UI understanding and code generation capabilities.  Experiments demonstrate that ScreenCoder achieves state-of-the-art performance in terms of layout accuracy, structural coherence, and code correctness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:
    *   **Modular Multi-Agent Framework:** Decomposing UI-to-code generation into distinct grounding, planning, and generation stages is a significant architectural improvement, enabling better interpretability, robustness, and domain knowledge integration. This is a clear departure from end-to-end black-box approaches.
    *   **Domain Knowledge Integration:** The planning agent explicitly incorporates front-end engineering priors, a crucial element often lacking in general-purpose VLMs. This allows for better layout adherence and code structure.
    *   **Data Engine for VLM Training:** The system's ability to automatically generate large-scale UI-code datasets and use them for fine-tuning and reinforcement learning of an existing VLM is a valuable contribution. This addresses the data scarcity problem in this domain and provides a practical path for improving model alignment.
    *   **Adaptive Prompting:** Generating adaptive prompts based on semantic identity, layout context, and user instructions.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from:
    *   **Improved Performance:** Achieving state-of-the-art results highlights the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Practicality:** The focus on realistic design workflows (starting from visual sketches) increases the practical relevance of the system.
    *   **Scalability:** The data engine aspect makes the framework scalable and adaptable, paving the way for continuous improvement and personalized UI code assistants.
    *   **Interpretability:** The modular design allows users to understand and potentially influence the generation process, enabling human-in-the-loop workflows.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the methodology.
    *   The modular design is a significant advantage.
    *   The use of an open-source VLM (Qwen2.5-VL) is commendable.
    *   The experiments appear to be thorough, demonstrating the effectiveness of the approach across various metrics.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper does not deeply analyze the types of UI designs where ScreenCoder excels or fails. A more detailed error analysis would be valuable.
    *   The data generation process, while automated, is still limited by the framework itself. There could be biases in the types of UI designs generated.
    *   Although the system supports user instructions, the extent of customizability and the types of design modifications possible through natural language are not fully explored.
    *   The paper does not discuss the computational cost of the multi-agent approach compared to end-to-end methods.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in UI generation, multimodal learning, and program synthesis. The modular design and data engine concept can inspire other researchers to develop more structured and scalable systems. It also sets a higher performance bar for future UI-to-code generation models.

*   **Score Justification:**

I am assigning a score of **8**.  The paper presents a well-designed and implemented framework that significantly advances the state-of-the-art in UI-to-code generation. The modular multi-agent architecture, integration of domain knowledge, and scalable data engine represent significant improvements over existing methods. The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach. While there are some limitations (as outlined above), the paper's novelty, significance, and potential influence on the field justify a high score. The key differentiators contributing to this score are the decomposition into interpretable stages which promotes robust component recognition, intelligent layout planning, and structured code generation, compared to the black-box approach used in previous works.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Repair-R1: Better Test Before Repair
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.22853v1)
- **Authors**: Haichuan Hu, Xiaochen Xie, Quanjun Zhang
- **Abstract**: APR (Automated Program Repair) aims to automatically locate program defects, generate patches and validate the repairs. Existing techniques for APR are often combined with LLMs (Large Language Models), which leverages the code-related knowledge of LLMs to improve repair effectiveness. Current LLM-based APR methods typically utilize test cases only during the inference stage, adopting an iterative approach that performs repair first and validates it through test execution afterward. This conventional paradigm neglects two important aspects: the potential contribution of test cases in the training phase, and the possibility of leveraging testing prior to repair. To address this, we propose Repair-R1, which introduces test cases into the model's training phase and shifts test generation to precede repair. The model is required to first generate discriminative test cases that can distinguish defective behaviors, and then perform repair based on these tests. This enables the model to better locate defects and understand the underlying causes of defects, thereby improving repair effectiveness. We implement Repair-R1 with three different backbone models, using RL (reinforcement learning) to co-optimize test generation and bug repair. Experimental results on four widely adopted benchmarks demonstrate the superiority of Repair-R1. Specially, compared to vanilla models, Repair-R1 improves repair success rate by 2.68\% to 48.29\%, test generation success rate by 16.38\% to 53.28\%, and test coverage by 0.78\% to 53.96\%. We publish the code and weights at https://github.com/Tomsawyerhu/APR-RL and https://huggingface.co/tomhu/Qwen3-4B-RL-5000-step.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper. Here's a summary, followed by a critical evaluation and a score.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Repair-R1, a novel approach to automated program repair (APR) that integrates test case generation into the model's training phase, preceding the repair process itself.  Unlike existing LLM-based APR methods that primarily use test cases for post-repair validation, Repair-R1 uses reinforcement learning (RL) to train a model to *first* generate discriminative test cases that expose bugs, and *then* generate repairs based on those test cases. The authors argue that this approach enables the model to better understand the underlying causes of defects, leading to more effective repairs. They implement Repair-R1 with three different backbone LLMs and evaluate it on four widely adopted benchmarks, demonstrating significant improvements in repair success rate, test generation success rate, and test coverage compared to vanilla and fine-tuned models. The authors also provide ablation studies and comparisons to single-objective optimization methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of integrating test generation *before* repair into the training loop, and using RL to co-optimize both aspects, is genuinely novel.  Existing approaches primarily use test cases as a validation mechanism *after* repair. Shifting the paradigm to proactive test generation represents a departure from the conventional test-driven approach in LLM-based APR. The use of GRPO is not necessarily novel in itself, but the application within this specific co-optimization framework and the reformulation to align with ELBO is interesting.

* **Significance:** The paper demonstrates tangible improvements in repair performance across several benchmarks, which is significant.  The results suggest that understanding the bug *before* attempting a fix (facilitated by the generated test cases) does indeed lead to better repairs. The ablation studies further support the importance of the joint optimization strategy. The paper's findings could influence the design of future APR systems by highlighting the value of incorporating test generation directly into the training process. This could lead to more robust and effective APR tools.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing LLM-based APR methods.
    * **Novel Approach:** The proposed Repair-R1 is a distinct and innovative solution.
    * **Strong Empirical Evaluation:** The experiments are comprehensive, covering multiple models, benchmarks, and ablation studies. The reported improvements are statistically significant and practically relevant.
    * **Well-Written:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow.
    * **Reproducibility:** The authors provide links to the code and weights, enhancing reproducibility.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational Cost:** The paper doesn't explicitly address the computational cost associated with RL training and test generation.  RL is known to be computationally expensive, and the added step of test generation could further increase the training time and resource requirements. This needs to be explicitly addressed.
    * **Scalability:** While the paper shows improvements on existing benchmarks, the scalability of Repair-R1 to larger, more complex software projects remains an open question. The paper doesn't address this point, though it is a natural question for follow up work.
    * **Reward Shaping:** The reward shaping in RL can be a delicate process. While the paper explains the reward design, there is no discussion of how sensitive the performance is to variations in the reward parameters. A more thorough analysis of the reward function's impact would strengthen the paper.
    * **Reliance on Existing Benchmarks:** The reliance on standard bug repair benchmarks is both a strength and a weakness. While this facilitates comparison, the field may benefit from testing on more "real-world" and varied datasets.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to shift the focus of LLM-based APR research towards proactive test generation.  It could inspire new approaches that combine test generation and repair in more integrated ways. The RL framework could also be adapted and extended by other researchers in the field.

**Justification for Score:**

While Repair-R1 presents a novel and promising approach to APR with solid empirical validation, the computational cost and potential scalability limitations need to be more thoroughly addressed. The reliance on benchmarks, while necessary for comparison, limits the assessment of real-world impact.  The innovation and solid results, however, justify a positive evaluation.

### Synchronization of mean-field models on the circle
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.22857v1)
- **Authors**: Yury Polyanskiy, Philippe Rigollet, Andrew Yao
- **Abstract**: This paper considers a mean-field model of $n$ interacting particles whose state space is the unit circle, a generalization of the classical Kuramoto model. Global synchronization is said to occur if after starting from almost any initial state, all particles coalesce to a common point on the circle. We propose a general synchronization criterion in terms of $L_1$-norm of the third derivative of the particle interaction function. As an application we resolve a conjecture for the so-called self-attention dynamics (stylized model of transformers), by showing synchronization for all $\beta \ge -0.16$, which significantly extends the previous bound of $0\le \beta \le 1$ from Criscitiello, Rebjock, McRae, and Boumal (2024). We also show that global synchronization does not occur when $\beta < -2/3$.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the synchronization of mean-field models on the circle, generalizing the classical Kuramoto model. The authors establish a general synchronization criterion based on the L1-norm of the third derivative of the particle interaction function. As an application, they resolve a conjecture regarding self-attention dynamics (a simplified model of transformers), demonstrating synchronization for a wider range of parameters than previously known.  Specifically, they extend the proven range of synchronization for the self-attention model to β ≥ −0.16, significantly improving upon the prior bound of 0 ≤ β < 1. They also prove that synchronization does *not* occur for β < -2/3.  The paper also considers a normalized version of these dynamics, relevant to transformers, and extends the criteria to certain non-mean-field systems where particles are aggregated with unequal weights.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the understanding of synchronization phenomena in mean-field models on the circle, particularly in the context of self-attention dynamics relevant to transformer networks.

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the development of a general synchronization criterion based on the L1-norm of the third derivative of the interaction function. This provides a tool for analyzing synchronization in a broader class of models than previously tractable. Resolving the conjecture regarding self-attention dynamics for a much larger range of β values is also a notable achievement. The extension to normalized dynamics and the unequal weight scenario provides additional value, increasing the generality of the results.  The proof techniques, while building on existing work (e.g., Łojasiewicz's theorem and the center-stable manifold theorem),  require novel adaptations and insights to apply to this specific problem.

*   **Significance:**  Understanding synchronization in these models is relevant to understanding the behavior of transformers, particularly phenomena like clustering and oversmoothing. The paper offers theoretical insights into how model parameters affect the tendency for particles to coalesce, which can be helpful for designing and analyzing transformer architectures. The result that synchronization *doesn't* occur when β < -2/3 also provides a concrete theoretical boundary and prevents further wasted effort searching in that parameter range.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Rigorous mathematical analysis.
    *   Clear statement of the main results and their implications.
    *   Significant extension of existing knowledge regarding the synchronization behavior of self-attention dynamics.
    *   General synchronization criterion applicable to a broader class of models.
    *   Well-organized structure and clear presentation of technical details.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper provides a more general criterion, it still relies on certain assumptions (e.g., real-analyticity in Theorem 2.3).
    *   The paper's focus is primarily theoretical. While the connection to transformer behavior is discussed, the paper doesn't provide empirical validation or demonstrate a direct practical impact on transformer design.
    *   The proofs are quite technical, potentially making the results inaccessible to a broader audience.
    *   The lower bound on beta is still an open question within the bounds discovered (β∈ (-3, -0.25).

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely be of interest to researchers in theoretical machine learning, dynamical systems, and mathematical physics. The new synchronization criterion could be used to analyze other related models, and the improved understanding of self-attention dynamics could inform future research on transformers.
While it has important implications within the field, it does not immediately present a paradigm shifting transformation of how attention models are developed.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's novelty is significant, especially in the context of expanding synchronization knowledge for a transformer model. Furthermore, the result extends the range of known synchronization parameters by orders of magnitude. It also adds in a novel parameter range where synchronization does *not* occur. The general criterion provides value beyond the specific application to self-attention dynamics. However, the work remains largely theoretical, and the complexity of the proofs might limit its accessibility.

### Automatically discovering heuristics in a complex SAT solver with large language models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.22876v1)
- **Authors**: Yiwen Sun, Furong Ye, Zhihan Chen, Ke Wei, Shaowei Cai
- **Abstract**: Satisfiability problem (SAT) is a cornerstone of computational complexity with broad industrial applications, and it remains challenging to optimize modern SAT solvers in real-world settings due to their intricate architectures. While automatic configuration frameworks have been developed, they rely on manually constrained search spaces and yield limited performance gains. This work introduces a novel paradigm which effectively optimizes complex SAT solvers via Large Language Models (LLMs), and a tool called AutoModSAT is developed. Three fundamental challenges are addressed in order to achieve superior performance: (1) LLM-friendly solver: Systematic guidelines are proposed for developing a modularized solver to meet LLMs' compatibility, emphasizing code simplification, information share and bug reduction; (2) Automatic prompt optimization: An unsupervised automatic prompt optimization method is introduced to advance the diversity of LLMs' output; (3) Efficient search strategy: We design a presearch strategy and an EA evolutionary algorithm for the final efficient and effective discovery of heuristics. Extensive experiments across a wide range of datasets demonstrate that AutoModSAT achieves 50% performance improvement over the baseline solver and achieves 30% superiority against the state-of-the-art (SOTA) solvers. Moreover, AutoModSAT attains a 20% speedup on average compared to parameter-tuned alternatives of the SOTA solvers, showcasing the enhanced capability in handling complex problem instances. This work bridges the gap between AI-driven heuristics discovery and mission-critical system optimization, and provides both methodological advancements and empirically validated results for next-generation complex solver development.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Automatically discovering heuristics in a complex SAT solver with large language models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AutoModSAT, a novel framework leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically optimize complex SAT solvers. The framework addresses key challenges: creating an LLM-friendly, modular solver (ModSAT), automating prompt optimization via entropy maximization, and employing an efficient search strategy combining pre-search with an evolutionary algorithm.  The authors demonstrate that AutoModSAT achieves significant performance improvements compared to baseline and state-of-the-art (SOTA) SAT solvers across various datasets, highlighting its potential to discover heuristics beyond human-designed search spaces. The work bridges the gap between AI-driven heuristics discovery and mission-critical system optimization for complex solvers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The idea of using LLMs for algorithm configuration is not entirely new. Previous work has explored LLMs for mathematical problem solving and designing simpler algorithms.  However, applying LLMs to optimize a *complex* solver like a modern SAT solver, with its intricate data structures and large codebase, is relatively novel. The specific approach of creating a modular solver (ModSAT) to make it LLM-compatible, automating prompt optimization, and the specific pre-search strategy are further innovations. The combination of these techniques to tackle a real-world solver optimization problem distinguishes this work.

**Significance:**

The potential impact of this work is significant.  Optimizing complex solvers is a challenging and important problem with broad applications. AutoModSAT offers a promising automated approach that can potentially reduce the need for manual tuning by experts, particularly for specific applications. The performance improvements demonstrated in the experiments are substantial, suggesting that AutoModSAT can indeed lead to better solvers. The development of guidelines for creating LLM-friendly solvers can also be valuable for other complex software systems.
The modular approach is a necessary adaptation for effectively working with LLMs within length constraints but reduces the performance of the baseline solver.

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Relevance:** Addresses a highly relevant and challenging problem in SAT solving.
*   **Novel Approach:** Integrates LLMs, modular solver design, automatic prompt optimization, and evolutionary search in a unique way.
*   **Empirical Validation:**  Extensive experiments across diverse datasets demonstrate significant performance gains.
*   **LLM-Friendly Design Principles:** Proposes valuable guidelines for modularizing complex solvers for LLM compatibility.
*   **Automation:** Reduces the labor-intensive process of manual solver tuning.
* **Identifies Novel Heuristics:** The analysis of discovered heuristics provides valuable insights into solver design.
* **Demonstrated Speedup:** AutoModSAT also attains a 20% speedup on average compared to parameter-tuned alternatives of SOTA solvers, further enhancing the capability in complex problem instances.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLMs:** The framework's performance heavily relies on the capabilities of the underlying LLM. Improvements in LLMs will directly translate to improvements in AutoModSAT, however it is restricted by the LLM performance.
*   **Modularity Trade-off:** The modularization of the SAT solver may introduce some performance overhead compared to highly optimized, non-modular solvers. (Performance of baselines vs modsat)
*   **Computational Cost:**  Training AutoModSAT and running the evolutionary search can be computationally expensive. Need for a 5000 timeout
*   **Generalizability Concerns**:  The performance of AutoModSAT on unseen problem instances (beyond the training datasets) needs further investigation. It would be useful to assess how easily heuristics developed on one domain transfer to another, or whether it requires re-training.
*   **Overfitting Potential**: The optimization process could potentially lead to overfitting on the training data. The authors mention mitigating this, but further analysis is needed. The selection of parameter ranges for baseline tuning may also present risks for overfitting.
*   **Limited Comparison:** The comparison of LLM discovered heuristics with pre-existing, and manually-designed heuristics could be expanded.
*   **Explanations on implementation detail:** Clarity around the implementation decisions surrounding the modularizations would be helpful.
*The limited information sharing and bug reduction techniques will inevitably impact code quality.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable and promising approach to automatically optimizing complex SAT solvers using LLMs. While the idea of using LLMs for algorithm design has been explored before, the application to a complex solver like a modern SAT solver, combined with the modularization strategy and automatic prompt optimization, constitutes a novel contribution. The empirical results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of AutoModSAT.
Despite these accomplishments, limitations surrounding computational requirements and a somewhat constrained benchmark dataset constrain a higher assessment of its potential contribution.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Score**: 7/10

### RecGPT Technical Report
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.22879v2)
- **Authors**: Chao Yi, Dian Chen, Gaoyang Guo, Jiakai Tang, Jian Wu, Jing Yu, Mao Zhang, Sunhao Dai, Wen Chen, Wenjun Yang, Yuning Jiang, Zhujin Gao, Bo Zheng, Chi Li, Dimin Wang, Dixuan Wang, Fan Li, Fan Zhang, Haibin Chen, Haozhuang Liu, Jialin Zhu, Jiamang Wang, Jiawei Wu, Jin Cui, Ju Huang, Kai Zhang, Kan Liu, Lang Tian, Liang Rao, Longbin Li, Lulu Zhao, Na He, Peiyang Wang, Qiqi Huang, Tao Luo, Wenbo Su, Xiaoxiao He, Xin Tong, Xu Chen, Xunke Xi, Yang Li, Yaxuan Wu, Yeqiu Yang, Yi Hu, Yinnan Song, Yuchen Li, Yujie Luo, Yujin Yuan, Yuliang Yan, Zhengyang Wang, Zhibo Xiao, Zhixin Ma, Zile Zhou, Ziqi Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recommender systems are among the most impactful applications of artificial intelligence, serving as critical infrastructure connecting users, merchants, and platforms. However, most current industrial systems remain heavily reliant on historical co-occurrence patterns and log-fitting objectives, i.e., optimizing for past user interactions without explicitly modeling user intent. This log-fitting approach often leads to overfitting to narrow historical preferences, failing to capture users' evolving and latent interests. As a result, it reinforces filter bubbles and long-tail phenomena, ultimately harming user experience and threatening the sustainability of the whole recommendation ecosystem. To address these challenges, we rethink the overall design paradigm of recommender systems and propose RecGPT, a next-generation framework that places user intent at the center of the recommendation pipeline. By integrating large language models (LLMs) into key stages of user interest mining, item retrieval, and explanation generation, RecGPT transforms log-fitting recommendation into an intent-centric process. To effectively align general-purpose LLMs to the above domain-specific recommendation tasks at scale, RecGPT incorporates a multi-stage training paradigm, which integrates reasoning-enhanced pre-alignment and self-training evolution, guided by a Human-LLM cooperative judge system. Currently, RecGPT has been fully deployed on the Taobao App. Online experiments demonstrate that RecGPT achieves consistent performance gains across stakeholders: users benefit from increased content diversity and satisfaction, merchants and the platform gain greater exposure and conversions. These comprehensive improvement results across all stakeholders validates that LLM-driven, intent-centric design can foster a more sustainable and mutually beneficial recommendation ecosystem.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the RecGPT Technical Report:

**Summary:**

The RecGPT Technical Report introduces RecGPT, a novel framework for recommender systems that integrates large language models (LLMs) to explicitly model user intent.  Instead of relying solely on historical co-occurrence patterns and log-fitting objectives, RecGPT uses LLMs for user interest mining, item tag prediction, and explanation generation.  The system consists of three LLM components: one for identifying user interests from behavior history (LLMUI), one for predicting relevant item tags (LLMIT), and one for generating personalized explanations (LLMRE). A multi-stage training process, including reasoning-enhanced pre-alignment and self-training evolution guided by a Human-LLM cooperative judge system, is used to adapt general-purpose LLMs to these domain-specific tasks.  The framework is deployed on Taobao, and online A/B testing results indicate substantial improvements in user engagement, commercial conversion, and platform health. The system also appears to effectively mitigate the Matthew effect, promoting a more equitable exposure of products from different merchants. The paper detailed techniques and protocols implemented to address challenges such as limited context window, lack of domain knowledge, and temporal misalignment to improve the stability and reliability of the LLM-based recommendation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The integration of LLMs into a production-scale recommender system as described in RecGPT represents a significant advancement. While LLMs have been explored in recommendation research, deploying a system that serves over a billion users and items is novel. The use of LLMs not just for improving recommendations but also for generating explanations is a valuable addition. The multi-stage training process, including the Human-LLM cooperative judge system, is a practical solution to the challenges of adapting LLMs to the recommendation domain. The use of tag-aware semantic retrieval in item retrieval has some novelty.

*   **Significance:** The work demonstrates the potential of LLMs to address the limitations of traditional log-fitting recommender systems, especially filter bubbles and the Matthew effect. By explicitly modeling user intent, RecGPT can provide more diverse and relevant recommendations, leading to improved user satisfaction and a healthier marketplace ecosystem. The comprehensive A/B testing results provide strong evidence of the system's effectiveness. A key significance of this report lies in the clear articulation of the challenges inherent in deploying LLMs in large-scale industrial recommender systems and practical solutions to overcome these difficulties.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Production Scale Deployment:** Demonstrating LLM integration in a large-scale, real-world recommender system is a major strength.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The A/B testing results cover a wide range of metrics across users, merchants, and the platform.
    *   **Practical Solutions:** The paper addresses specific challenges in adapting LLMs to the recommendation domain with concrete techniques.
    *   **Clear Articulation of Challenges:** The discussion of problems such as cognitive bias in LLM judges and temporal misalignment is insightful.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Technical Detail:** While the paper outlines the framework, it lacks some specific details on the LLM architectures, training hyperparameters, and implementation choices. Providing more specifics would allow for easier replication by other researchers.
    *   **Generalizability:** The results are specific to Taobao and its user base. While the general principles may apply to other platforms, the specific implementation details may need to be adapted.
    *   **Black Box Nature:** While LLMs bring many positive aspects, they introduce complexity in understanding and debugging the model.  More insight is needed on how to interpret the behavior of RecGPT.
    *   **Reliance on Internal Resources:** The use of the proprietary TBStars model limits external verification.

*   **Potential Influence:**  This work will likely influence the design of future recommender systems and accelerate the adoption of LLMs in this field. The techniques for adapting LLMs to recommendation tasks, particularly the multi-stage training process and the Human-LLM cooperative judge system, will be valuable to other researchers and practitioners. The reported performance improvements and the mitigation of the Matthew effect will motivate further research in this direction.

Score: 8

**Rationale:**  The RecGPT Technical Report describes a highly significant advancement in recommender system design by effectively integrating large language models at scale. While the paper has some limitations related to missing specific technical details and the use of a proprietary model, the practical success, comprehensive evaluation, and potential impact on the field justify a high score. The integration of LLMs and the deployment at the scale described make it one of the most important papers in this area. The demonstrated real-world impact and the discussion of challenges and solutions give it considerable practical value.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 8/10

### AUV-Fusion: Cross-Modal Adversarial Fusion of User Interactions and Visual Perturbations Against VARS
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.22880v1)
- **Authors**: Hai Ling, Tianchi Wang, Xiaohao Liu, Zhulin Tao, Lifang Yang, Xianglin Huang
- **Abstract**: Modern Visual-Aware Recommender Systems (VARS) exploit the integration of user interaction data and visual features to deliver personalized recommendations with high precision. However, their robustness against adversarial attacks remains largely underexplored, posing significant risks to system reliability and security. Existing attack strategies suffer from notable limitations: shilling attacks are costly and detectable, and visual-only perturbations often fail to align with user preferences. To address these challenges, we propose AUV-Fusion, a cross-modal adversarial attack framework that adopts high-order user preference modeling and cross-modal adversary generation. Specifically, we obtain robust user embeddings through multi-hop user-item interactions and transform them via an MLP into semantically aligned perturbations. These perturbations are injected onto the latent space of a pre-trained VAE within the diffusion model. By synergistically integrating genuine user interaction data with visually plausible perturbations, AUV-Fusion eliminates the need for injecting fake user profiles and effectively mitigates the challenge of insufficient user preference extraction inherent in traditional visual-only attacks. Comprehensive evaluations on diverse VARS architectures and real-world datasets demonstrate that AUV-Fusion significantly enhances the exposure of target (cold-start) items compared to conventional baseline methods. Moreover, AUV-Fusion maintains exceptional stealth under rigorous scrutiny.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AUV-Fusion, a novel cross-modal adversarial attack framework targeting Visual-Aware Recommender Systems (VARS).  Unlike existing attacks that focus on either shilling attacks (costly, detectable) or visual-only perturbations (lacking user preference alignment), AUV-Fusion combines user-item interaction dynamics with visual perturbations to achieve stealthy and effective recommendation poisoning.  It does this by using a high-order user preference modeling module (leveraging a graph convolutional network) and a cross-modal adversary generation module (incorporating user-driven perturbations into a diffusion model). The framework perturbs item images to promote target (cold-start) items in recommendations, while maintaining visual plausibility and user alignment. Extensive experiments on VARS architectures and datasets demonstrate the effectiveness and stealth of AUV-Fusion compared to baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *cross-modal approach*, blending user-item interaction data and visual perturbations in a principled way. The integration of high-order user preference modeling using a graph convolutional network to guide the adversarial image generation via a diffusion model is also a notable contribution. Existing methods mostly focus on uni-modal attacks on visual features or shilling attacks, making AUV-Fusion a significant departure.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical vulnerability of VARS, which are becoming increasingly prevalent. By demonstrating the susceptibility of VARS to cross-modal attacks, the paper highlights a security gap that needs attention. Boosting cold-start items is a practical scenario, making the attack relevant. The demonstration of improved attack performance *and* stealth is valuable.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The cross-modal approach is well-motivated and tackles limitations of existing attacks.
    *   The use of a diffusion model allows for the generation of visually plausible perturbations.
    *   The high-order user preference modeling is a key component in ensuring effective and user-aligned attacks.
    *   Extensive experiments on multiple datasets and VARS architectures. The analysis of "exposure gain" and "attack imperceptibility" is comprehensive.
    *   Ablation studies clearly demonstrate the importance of the components of the framework.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The attack is still evaluated under a black-box setting with partial interaction data access. Although this is more realistic than having complete knowledge of the system, exploring white-box or gray-box settings would strengthen the analysis further.
    *   While the human study assesses overall imperceptibility, a deeper analysis of *semantic* imperceptibility in the user study would be beneficial. It's possible the perturbations are visually subtle but change user perception in more significant ways.
    *   The reliance on pre-trained models (VAE, feature extractor) introduces potential biases. While they mention off-the-shelf model use and lack of fine-tuning, more discussion on the implications of this is important, especially for generalizability.
    *   The defense against this attack is not discussed in detail. Identifying potential defense mechanisms based on the insights from AUV-Fusion would significantly increase the impact.
*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could stimulate further research into cross-modal adversarial attacks on recommendation systems and the development of more robust VARS architectures. The AUV-Fusion framework provides a strong foundation for future work exploring more sophisticated attack strategies and defense mechanisms.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper is well-written and technically sound, some aspects require more depth and considerations, such as defence mechanism, and the model bias.  The novelty and impact are significant due to the well-motivated cross-modal approach and the successful combination of user preference modeling and visual perturbation. A good contribution, but there are ways to refine it.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Where to show Demos in Your Prompt: A Positional Bias of In-Context Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.22887v1)
- **Authors**: Kwesi Cobbina, Tianyi Zhou
- **Abstract**: In-context learning (ICL) is a critical emerging capability of large language models (LLMs), enabling few-shot learning during inference by including a few demonstrations (demos) in the prompt. However, it has been found that ICL's performance can be sensitive to the choices of demos and their order. This paper investigates an unexplored new positional bias of ICL for the first time: we observe that the predictions and accuracy can drift drastically when the positions of demos, the system prompt, and the user message in LLM input are varied. We refer to this bias as DEMOS' POSITION IN PROMPT (DPP) bias. We design a systematic evaluation pipeline to study this type of positional bias across classification, question answering, summarization, and reasoning tasks. We introduce two metrics, ACCURACY-CHANGE and PREDICTION-CHANGE, to quantify net gains and output volatility induced by changes in the demos' position. Extensive experiments on ten LLMs from four open-source model families (QWEN, LLAMA3, MISTRAL, COHERE) verify that the bias significantly affects their accuracy and predictions: placing demos at the start of the prompt yields the most stable and accurate outputs with gains of up to +6 points. In contrast, placing demos at the end of the user message flips over 30\% of predictions without improving correctness on QA tasks. Smaller models are most affected by this sensitivity, though even large models remain marginally affected on complex tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces and investigates a novel positional bias in in-context learning (ICL) called "Demos' Position In Prompt" (DPP) bias.  The authors observe that the placement of demonstrations within a prompt (relative to the system prompt and user message) significantly affects the accuracy and prediction stability of large language models (LLMs).  They systematically evaluate this bias across eight tasks, ten LLMs (from four model families), and four canonical demo placements (start/end of system prompt, start/end of user message).  They introduce metrics, Accuracy-Change and Prediction-Change, to quantify these positional effects. Their results show that placing demos early in the prompt (start of system prompt or end of system prompt) generally leads to more stable and accurate outputs, while placing demos later (end of user message) can degrade performance and increase prediction volatility. The sensitivity varies by model size and task complexity, with smaller models being more affected. They conclude that prompt design requires careful consideration of demonstration placement, tailored to the specific model and task.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The discovery and systematic investigation of DPP bias is a significant contribution. While prior work acknowledged prompt sensitivity, this paper isolates and quantifies the specific impact of demo *position* relative to prompt roles (system/user), a factor largely unexplored. This is a new and important aspect of ICL that has implications for prompt engineering.
*   **Thoroughness:** The experimental design is robust. The authors considered a diverse set of tasks (classification, QA, summarization, reasoning), models (spanning different architectures and sizes), and demo placements.  The introduction of Accuracy-Change and Prediction-Change metrics provides a richer understanding of performance shifts beyond simple accuracy.
*   **Practical Implications:** The findings have direct practical relevance for anyone using LLMs with ICL.  The recommendations to evaluate demo placement and to consider positional robustness in prompt engineering pipelines are immediately useful.
*   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the problem, methodology, results, and implications. The figures are helpful in visualizing the key findings.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Mechanistic Explanation:** While the paper presents compelling empirical evidence, the explanations for why DPP bias occurs are somewhat speculative.  The architectural and data-driven hypotheses are plausible, but further mechanistic investigation is needed to fully understand the underlying causes.  Connecting these findings more directly to transformer attention patterns or specific instruction-tuning dataset characteristics would strengthen the analysis.
*   **Limited Scope of Mitigation Strategies:** The suggested mitigation strategies (test-time calibration, permuted context pre-training) are preliminary and not evaluated experimentally in this paper.  More in-depth exploration of effective mitigation techniques would enhance the paper's practical value.
*   **English Language Focus:**  The experiments are limited to English datasets.  The authors acknowledge that cross-lingual variations could lead to different results, but further investigation would be useful to prove this point.
*   **Metric limitations** The study is reliant on accuracy metrics.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a critical issue in ICL: the sensitivity to prompt structure. By uncovering DPP bias, the authors challenge the assumption that LLMs learn robustly from any properly formatted context. This discovery calls for more rigorous prompt engineering practices and raises fundamental questions about the nature of learning in LLMs. While it may seem incremental, ICL brittleness is a large problem and a robust effort to characterise a novel source of brittleness is significant.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**. While the paper's mechanistic explanations and mitigation strategies are less fully developed, the discovery and systematic characterization of DPP bias are highly valuable. The thorough experimental design, novel metrics, and practical implications make it a significant contribution to the field of in-context learning. The paper will likely stimulate further research into understanding and mitigating positional biases in LLMs. It is a solid piece of empirical work that provides a substantial contribution to a critical area in NLP.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### C3: A Bilingual Benchmark for Spoken Dialogue Models Exploring Challenges in Complex Conversations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.22968v1)
- **Authors**: Chengqian Ma, Wei Tao, Yiwen Guo
- **Abstract**: Spoken Dialogue Models (SDMs) have recently attracted significant attention for their ability to generate voice responses directly to users' spoken queries. Despite their increasing popularity, there exists a gap in research focused on comprehensively understanding their practical effectiveness in comprehending and emulating human conversations. This is especially true compared to text-based Large Language Models (LLMs), which benefit from extensive benchmarking. Human voice interactions are inherently more complex than text due to characteristics unique to spoken dialogue. Ambiguity poses one challenge, stemming from semantic factors like polysemy, as well as phonological aspects such as heterograph, heteronyms, and stress patterns. Additionally, context-dependency, like omission, coreference, and multi-turn interaction, adds further complexity to human conversational dynamics. To illuminate the current state of SDM development and to address these challenges, we present a benchmark dataset in this paper, which comprises 1,079 instances in English and Chinese. Accompanied by an LLM-based evaluation method that closely aligns with human judgment, this dataset facilitates a comprehensive exploration of the performance of SDMs in tackling these practical challenges.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces C³, a bilingual (English and Chinese) benchmark dataset designed to evaluate the performance of Spoken Dialogue Models (SDMs) in handling complex conversations. It focuses on challenges like phonological and semantic ambiguity, omission, coreference, and multi-turn interaction, which are often overlooked in existing benchmarks. The authors also propose an LLM-based evaluation method and assess six popular SDMs, providing insights into their strengths and weaknesses across different phenomena and languages. They found ambiguity is more difficult for SDMs, processing omission is the most difficult in context-dependency and complex dialogues in Chinese are more difficult.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation of a bilingual (English & Chinese) dataset that explicitly targets conversational complexities often ignored in existing SDM benchmarks. While prior benchmarks exist for SDMs, few comprehensively address ambiguity (phonological and semantic), coreference, omission, and multi-turn interactions in a single dataset and across two languages. The LLM-based evaluation method, while not entirely new in concept (LLM-as-a-judge), is adapted and specifically designed for the nuances of spoken dialogue evaluation which contributes to the novelty aspect.

**Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the increasing importance of SDMs in human-computer interaction. By highlighting specific challenges faced by SDMs in realistically complex dialogues, the benchmark dataset facilitates more targeted research and development. The paper provides practical insights into the comparative strengths and weaknesses of different SDMs (GPT-40-Audio-Preview, Freeze-Omni, LLaMA-Omni, VITA-Audio, MooER-Omni, Qwen2.5-Omni), which can inform model selection and development strategies. Demonstrating the relative difficulty of certain phenomena like omission and semantic ambiguity provides researchers with concrete targets for improvement. Furthermore, the finding that SDMs perform better in English as compared to Chinese in most cases, raises questions of the cross-linguistic robustness of SDMs.

**Strengths:**
*   **Comprehensive Dataset Design:** The dataset is meticulously designed to capture different aspects of conversational complexity, addressing limitations in existing benchmarks. The use of real-world spoken dialogues enhances realism.
*   **Bilingual Approach:** Including both English and Chinese provides a broader perspective and allows for cross-lingual comparisons.
*   **LLM-Based Evaluation:** Using LLMs for automated evaluation aligns with current trends in NLP and offers a cost-effective way to assess model performance. The authors carefully validate the reliability of their LLM-based evaluation against human judgements.
*   **Practical Insights:** The paper offers valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different SDMs, providing actionable information for researchers and practitioners.

**Weaknesses:**
*   **Dataset Size:** While comprehensive in scope, the dataset size (1,079 instances) might be considered relatively small, especially compared to datasets used for training Large Language Models. This may limit the generalizability of the findings. More examples may prove more robust.
*   **Limited Model Comparison:** The analysis is limited to six specific SDMs. A broader comparative study of more SDMs would strengthen the conclusions.
*   **Data Generation/Enhancement:** The paper mentions using TTS to enhance the dataset. While reasonable, there is little information of quality or type of TTS used and how it could introduce biases (or potentially amplify biases).
*   **LLM Judge Limitations:** While the LLM judges are validated against human judges, potential inherent biases of LLMs are not explicitly addressed.

**Overall Assessment and Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of Spoken Dialogue Modeling by offering a well-designed benchmark dataset that tackles important conversational complexities and provides practical insights into the performance of existing SDMs. While there are minor weaknesses related to the size, model comparison, and potential bias in LLM judges, the strengths outweigh the shortcomings. The work fills a significant gap in existing research by focusing on the specific challenges of ambiguity, context-dependency, and cross-lingual performance. The benchmark has the potential to drive further research and development in more robust and human-like SDMs.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LesionGen: A Concept-Guided Diffusion Model for Dermatology Image Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.23001v1)
- **Authors**: Jamil Fayyad, Nourhan Bayasi, Ziyang Yu, Homayoun Najjaran
- **Abstract**: Deep learning models for skin disease classification require large, diverse, and well-annotated datasets. However, such resources are often limited due to privacy concerns, high annotation costs, and insufficient demographic representation. While text-to-image diffusion probabilistic models (T2I-DPMs) offer promise for medical data synthesis, their use in dermatology remains underexplored, largely due to the scarcity of rich textual descriptions in existing skin image datasets. In this work, we introduce LesionGen, a clinically informed T2I-DPM framework for dermatology image synthesis. Unlike prior methods that rely on simplistic disease labels, LesionGen is trained on structured, concept-rich dermatological captions derived from expert annotations and pseudo-generated, concept-guided reports. By fine-tuning a pretrained diffusion model on these high-quality image-caption pairs, we enable the generation of realistic and diverse skin lesion images conditioned on meaningful dermatological descriptions. Our results demonstrate that models trained solely on our synthetic dataset achieve classification accuracy comparable to those trained on real images, with notable gains in worst-case subgroup performance. Code and data are available here.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the LesionGen paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LesionGen, a novel concept-guided text-to-image diffusion probabilistic model (T2I-DPM) specifically designed for generating synthetic dermatology images.  The key contribution is the creation of high-quality image-caption pairs for training the T2I-DPM. This is achieved through two methods: (1) leveraging expert dermatological descriptions of images with structured metadata, and (2) generating pseudo-dermatological descriptions using a vision-language model (VLM) conditioned on the limited metadata available for other datasets. The fine-tuned T2I-DPM is then used to generate realistic and diverse skin lesion images, and the authors demonstrate that classifiers trained on this synthetic data achieve comparable or better performance (especially in worst-case scenarios) compared to classifiers trained on real-world datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable contribution by tackling the challenge of limited textual descriptions in dermatology image datasets, which is a significant hurdle for leveraging T2I-DPMs in this domain. LesionGen addresses this by generating structured, concept-rich captions that are paired with images for the diffusion model training. While other works have used diffusion models for dermatology, they have relied on simplistic label-based conditioning. The use of a VLM to generate dermatologically relevant text based on images and metadata is reasonably novel and adds significant value to the image generation pipeline. The combination of expert-derived descriptions and VLM-generated descriptions adds further depth.

*   **Significance:** The ability to generate synthetic medical images with realistic variations and representative of underrepresented groups has significant practical implications. It can alleviate data scarcity, reduce bias in AI models, and facilitate the development of robust and generalizable skin lesion classifiers. The improvement in worst-case subgroup performance is a particularly compelling outcome. The experimental results demonstrate that this method effectively improves performance on rare classes.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Concept-Driven Approach:** The use of clinically relevant concepts to guide both the caption generation and the image synthesis process is a major strength. This ensures that the generated images are not only visually realistic but also medically meaningful.
    *   **Dual-Description Method:** The combined use of structured metadata, expert descriptions, and VLM-generated descriptions makes the most of existing data resources.
    *   **Quantitative Results:** The rigorous experimental evaluation with quantitative metrics, comparing against strong baselines like real-only training and existing SOTA augmentation techniques. It demonstrates a tangible benefit.
    *   **Focus on Worst-Case Performance:**  The explicit targeting of improved performance in underrepresented subgroups is important and addresses a crucial need in medical image analysis.
    *   Code and Data availability: Making the code and data available fosters reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on VLM Quality:** The quality of the VLM-generated descriptions directly impacts the quality of the synthetic data. While the authors prompt the VLM appropriately, there's still a level of uncertainty in the descriptions. A more comprehensive analysis of the quality of the VLM outputs would be valuable.
    *   **Limited Skin Tone Diversity:** The paper acknowledges that expanding to skin tone diversity is future work. Addressing this limitation would further enhance the practical utility of the framework. The reliance on a single pre-trained diffusion model may also limit the generated diversity.
    *   **Small Dataset:** The D7P dataset is quite small, which makes the results difficult to generalize. While the authors address this by incorporating pseudo-labeled data from HAM10000, further experiments on larger datasets would strengthen the work.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of dermatology image analysis by enabling the development of more robust and generalizable AI models. It also demonstrates a promising approach for addressing data scarcity issues in other medical imaging domains where rich textual metadata is lacking.

**Justification for the Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign LesionGen a score of **8**.

The paper's contributions are significant in that it provides a novel approach to generate synthetic dermatology images that can be used to train more robust and generalizable AI models, particularly in worst-case performance scenarios. The concept-driven method is a marked improvement over prior approaches, and the quantitative results are compelling. However, the dependence on VLM quality, limited skin tone diversity, and the smaller dataset size of D7P prevent a higher score. The paper has a high degree of practical relevance and potential for further development and impact.
