### Stop Evaluating AI with Human Tests, Develop Principled, AI-specific Tests instead
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.23009v1)
- **Authors**: Tom Sühr, Florian E. Dorner, Olawale Salaudeen, Augustin Kelava, Samira Samadi
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable results on a range of standardized tests originally designed to assess human cognitive and psychological traits, such as intelligence and personality. While these results are often interpreted as strong evidence of human-like characteristics in LLMs, this paper argues that such interpretations constitute an ontological error. Human psychological and educational tests are theory-driven measurement instruments, calibrated to a specific human population. Applying these tests to non-human subjects without empirical validation, risks mischaracterizing what is being measured. Furthermore, a growing trend frames AI performance on benchmarks as measurements of traits such as ``intelligence'', despite known issues with validity, data contamination, cultural bias and sensitivity to superficial prompt changes. We argue that interpreting benchmark performance as measurements of human-like traits, lacks sufficient theoretical and empirical justification. This leads to our position: Stop Evaluating AI with Human Tests, Develop Principled, AI-specific Tests instead. We call for the development of principled, AI-specific evaluation frameworks tailored to AI systems. Such frameworks might build on existing frameworks for constructing and validating psychometrics tests, or could be created entirely from scratch to fit the unique context of AI.
- **Summary**: Okay, I have reviewed the provided paper. Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper argues against the common practice of evaluating AI systems, particularly Large Language Models (LLMs), using tests designed for humans, like personality inventories or standardized exams. The authors contend that such evaluations are fundamentally flawed because these tests are calibrated and validated for human populations, grounded in theories of human cognition and behavior. Applying them to LLMs without validation commits an ontological error, misinterpreting the meaning of the scores. The paper highlights issues like validity, data contamination, cultural bias, and sensitivity to prompt variations, arguing that benchmark performance shouldn't be directly equated with human-like traits like intelligence or personality. Instead, the authors advocate for developing principled, AI-specific evaluation frameworks tailored to the unique characteristics of AI systems, which might draw from psychometric principles but should be grounded in the realities of machine learning. They discuss the lack of measurement invariance when applying human tests to LLMs and the risk of redundant constructs. The paper concludes by suggesting future research directions for developing robust and valid measurement models for AI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's central argument – that human-centric tests are inappropriate for direct evaluation of AI – isn't entirely novel. However, it presents a comprehensive and well-articulated critique, bringing together arguments from various sources in a cohesive manner. The emphasis on the *ontological error* of direct comparison and the need for fundamentally AI-specific frameworks provides a specific and useful framing. The discussion of measurement invariance, nomological networks, and SEMs in the context of AI evaluation adds depth. Prior work has touched upon the limitations of benchmarks, but this paper focuses specifically on the fallacy of interpreting AI performance on human-designed tests as directly reflecting human-like traits.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it addresses a pervasive issue in the field of AI research. The tendency to anthropomorphize AI and equate its performance on human-designed tasks with human capabilities can lead to misleading interpretations, inflated expectations, and potentially flawed research directions. By clearly articulating the problems with this approach and proposing alternative frameworks, the paper can help shift the focus toward more rigorous and valid evaluation methods. The discussion of potential risks like false certification and obscured liability further underscores the importance of this critique. The outlined research directions for developing ML-specific traits are very useful.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper provides a strong and logically consistent argument.
    *   It clearly defines key concepts and uses examples to illustrate the issues.
    *   The discussion of measurement invariance is particularly insightful and relevant.
    *   It suggests constructive paths forward for developing better AI evaluation frameworks.
    *   The paper's review of related work, especially the discussion on the shift in language around benchmarks, is strong.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the paper identifies key issues, the proposed alternative frameworks are somewhat general. Further detailed suggestions for AI-specific metrics would strengthen the work. More concrete examples of how to apply concepts like nomological networks and SEMs to AI evaluation would also be beneficial.
    *   The paper is mostly theoretical. Empirical demonstrations of the pitfalls identified (e.g., showing how human-centric tests fail to predict AI behavior in real-world scenarios) would further bolster the argument.
    *   The paper could benefit from a deeper exploration of existing literature on AI evaluation, including methods that attempt to measure AI capabilities in a task-independent manner.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence how AI research is conducted and interpreted. By raising awareness of the limitations of human-centric evaluation, it can encourage researchers to adopt more rigorous and AI-specific approaches. It can also help to temper public expectations about AI capabilities and promote a more nuanced understanding of these systems.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

*   The paper makes a valuable contribution by critically examining a widespread practice in AI evaluation and proposing constructive alternatives. The problem identified is very relevant to the field and can help prevent misleading research in the future. However, while strong in its critique, the paper is somewhat limited by its theoretical nature and generality.
Score: 7
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Investigating the Invertibility of Multimodal Latent Spaces: Limitations of Optimization-Based Methods
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.23010v1)
- **Authors**: Siwoo Park
- **Abstract**: This paper investigates the inverse capabilities and broader utility of multimodal latent spaces within task-specific AI (Artificial Intelligence) models. While these models excel at their designed forward tasks (e.g., text-to-image generation, audio-to-text transcription), their potential for inverse mappings remains largely unexplored. We propose an optimization-based framework to infer input characteristics from desired outputs, applying it bidirectionally across Text-Image (BLIP, Flux.1-dev) and Text-Audio (Whisper-Large-V3, Chatterbox-TTS) modalities. Our central hypothesis posits that while optimization can guide models towards inverse tasks, their multimodal latent spaces will not consistently support semantically meaningful and perceptually coherent inverse mappings. Experimental results consistently validate this hypothesis. We demonstrate that while optimization can force models to produce outputs that align textually with targets (e.g., a text-to-image model generating an image that an image captioning model describes correctly, or an ASR model transcribing optimized audio accurately), the perceptual quality of these inversions is chaotic and incoherent. Furthermore, when attempting to infer the original semantic input from generative models, the reconstructed latent space embeddings frequently lack semantic interpretability, aligning with nonsensical vocabulary tokens. These findings highlight a critical limitation. multimodal latent spaces, primarily optimized for specific forward tasks, do not inherently possess the structure required for robust and interpretable inverse mappings. Our work underscores the need for further research into developing truly semantically rich and invertible multimodal latent spaces.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the invertibility of multimodal latent spaces in task-specific AI models. The authors explore whether these models, trained primarily for forward tasks like text-to-image generation or audio-to-text transcription, can effectively perform inverse mappings (e.g., inferring the text prompt from a generated image or audio).  They propose an optimization-based framework to attempt these inverse mappings across Text-Image (BLIP, Flux.1-dev) and Text-Audio (Whisper-Large-V3, Chatterbox-TTS) modalities.  The central finding is that while optimization can force the models to produce outputs that textually align with targets (e.g., generating an image whose caption matches the target text), the perceptual quality of these "inversions" is often chaotic and incoherent.  Furthermore, when trying to reconstruct semantic inputs from generative models, the reconstructed latent space embeddings lack semantic interpretability, often yielding nonsensical vocabulary tokens.  The authors conclude that multimodal latent spaces, optimized for forward tasks, don't inherently possess the structure needed for robust and interpretable inverse mappings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper addresses a relevant and increasingly important question about the limitations of current multimodal AI models.  The lack of invertibility in latent spaces, despite impressive forward task performance, raises concerns about the true "understanding" captured by these models.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:**  The optimization-based framework is a reasonable approach to explore the inverse mapping capabilities. Using gradient descent and established optimizers is a standard technique, making the methodology relatively sound and reproducible.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides empirical results across multiple modalities and models (BLIP, Flux.1-dev, Whisper-Large-V3, Chatterbox-TTS), strengthening the conclusions. Presenting log mel spectrograms and reconstructed waveforms for audio provide good visual representations.
    *   **Clear Hypothesis and Results:** The hypothesis is stated clearly, and the results, although negative (showing limitations), consistently validate the hypothesis.
    *   **Writing and Structure:** The paper is well-written, logically structured, and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty in Methodology:** The optimization-based approach is not particularly novel.  It leverages standard gradient-based optimization techniques, and the methodology is very similar to that used in adversarial attacks. It may not have been explicitly the *goal* of the method, but it is still very similar from an implementation perspective.
    *   **Incremental Contribution:** While the results are interesting and highlight a limitation, the contribution is somewhat incremental. The observation that models trained for specific forward tasks may struggle with the inverse is not entirely surprising, although the paper provides empirical evidence to support this intuition.
    *   **Limited Depth of Analysis:** The paper identifies the problem but doesn't offer substantial insights into *why* these latent spaces are not invertible or *how* to make them more so. The paper does not provide any solutions and just states the need for further research.
    *   **Qualitative Nature of Perceptual Incoherence:** The judgment of "perceptual quality being chaotic and incoherent" is largely subjective without a more formalized perceptual evaluation, such as user studies or established perceptual metrics.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to Stronger Baselines:** There are potentially other techniques to induce invertibility. While the paper focuses on an optimization-based approach, it doesn't compare the results with other potential strategies.

*   **Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its ability to highlight a critical limitation of modern multimodal AI models. While these models excel in the designed tasks, they appear to lack robust representational understanding that would allow for a direct inverse mapping from the output space to the input space in a meaningful way.  This has implications for the broader use of these models in applications where understanding the *reasoning* or *process* that leads to a particular output is important. The result highlights the need for developing better evaluation metrics that capture more than just forward performance.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution to the field by highlighting a practical limitation of widely used multimodal models, but the lack of novel methodologies and limited depth of analysis keeps it from being a more significant contribution. The findings are important, but incremental. Therefore, the score is balanced between the strengths (clear definition, empirical results) and weaknesses (incremental contribution, limited solutions).

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Modeling Human Gaze Behavior with Diffusion Models for Unified Scanpath Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.23021v1)
- **Authors**: Giuseppe Cartella, Vittorio Cuculo, Alessandro D'Amelio, Marcella Cornia, Giuseppe Boccignone, Rita Cucchiara
- **Abstract**: Predicting human gaze scanpaths is crucial for understanding visual attention, with applications in human-computer interaction, autonomous systems, and cognitive robotics. While deep learning models have advanced scanpath prediction, most existing approaches generate averaged behaviors, failing to capture the variability of human visual exploration. In this work, we present ScanDiff, a novel architecture that combines diffusion models with Vision Transformers to generate diverse and realistic scanpaths. Our method explicitly models scanpath variability by leveraging the stochastic nature of diffusion models, producing a wide range of plausible gaze trajectories. Additionally, we introduce textual conditioning to enable task-driven scanpath generation, allowing the model to adapt to different visual search objectives. Experiments on benchmark datasets show that ScanDiff surpasses state-of-the-art methods in both free-viewing and task-driven scenarios, producing more diverse and accurate scanpaths. These results highlight its ability to better capture the complexity of human visual behavior, pushing forward gaze prediction research. Source code and models are publicly available at https://aimagelab.github.io/ScanDiff.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the provided text.

**Summary:**

The paper presents ScanDiff, a novel architecture for predicting human gaze scanpaths.  It combines diffusion models with Vision Transformers to generate diverse and realistic scanpaths. ScanDiff explicitly models scanpath variability by leveraging the stochastic nature of diffusion models, producing a wide range of plausible gaze trajectories.  Furthermore, it incorporates textual conditioning to enable task-driven scanpath generation, allowing the model to adapt to different visual search objectives.  Experiments on benchmark datasets (COCO-FreeView, MIT1003, and COCO-Search18) demonstrate that ScanDiff surpasses state-of-the-art methods in both free-viewing and task-driven scenarios, producing more diverse and accurate scanpaths.  The paper also introduces a novel analysis of scanpath variability, showing that ScanDiff generates highly diverse eye trajectories that better capture human gaze behavior.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its integration of diffusion models with Vision Transformers for *unified* scanpath prediction across both free-viewing and task-driven scenarios. While diffusion models have been used in some prior gaze prediction work, these were generally limited to more constrained settings (e.g., 360° images, text). The specific architecture details (the ScanDiff architecture and how different components work together) also contribute to the novelty.
    The paper's introduction of textual conditioning for task-driven scanpath generation is also a novel and practically useful contribution. Most current systems for scanpath prediction cannot be controlled in such a way. The development of a new metric, Diversity-aware Sequence Score (DSS) , to assess the variability of predicted scanpaths, is another point of novelty, addressing a key limitation of previous evaluation approaches.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation of many existing scanpath prediction models: their tendency to generate averaged behaviors rather than capturing the inherent variability of human visual exploration. By explicitly modeling scanpath variability, ScanDiff provides a more realistic and potentially more useful simulation of human gaze. The paper also provides additional quantitative analysis, and a detailed breakdown of ScanDiff's performance.
    The state-of-the-art results across several benchmark datasets and the new analysis using the DSS metric provide strong empirical evidence of the paper's significance. Furthermore, the task-driven control via textual conditioning makes ScanDiff more applicable to real-world applications in areas such as human-computer interaction, autonomous systems, and cognitive robotics. It can inform how an AI agent might direct its gaze to gather information, for example.
    A point against significance: the models it beats, in particular for zero-shot evaluations in Table 6, appear not as relevant. It beats them strongly, but are those state of the art models worth considering?

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong empirical results on multiple datasets.
    *   Addresses a key limitation of prior work (lack of variability).
    *   Introduces textual conditioning for task-driven control.
    *   Provides a new metric (DSS) for evaluating scanpath diversity.
    *   Clear and well-written paper.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the integration of diffusion models is novel in *this specific context*, diffusion models are a well-established technique, so the algorithmic novelty may be considered incremental.
    *   The experimental evaluation, while comprehensive, could benefit from more in-depth analysis of the individual contributions of different architectural components (beyond the ablation studies). More user studies or examples related to the paper's applications in other fields may also be helpful in increasing the scope of the work.
    *   Some baseline results in Table 6 seem not very up-to-date.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of gaze prediction. Its emphasis on scanpath variability and task-driven control aligns well with the needs of many real-world applications. The DSS metric could become a standard tool for evaluating the diversity of gaze prediction models. The results presented, even if not fully groundbreaking, are strong enough to spur more work in the same area.
*   **Justification of Score:** Although the use of diffusion models is not completely new, the paper makes a significant contribution to the field of gaze prediction by integrating them effectively into a unified architecture, explicitly modeling scanpath variability, introducing textual conditioning, and providing strong empirical validation. The DSS metric is also a solid contribution. The weaknesses prevent a very high score, in particular on baseline models used and limited additional applications to real scenarios.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reference-Guided Diffusion Inpainting For Multimodal Counterfactual Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.23058v1)
- **Authors**: Alexandru Buburuzan
- **Abstract**: Safety-critical applications, such as autonomous driving and medical image analysis, require extensive multimodal data for rigorous testing. Synthetic data methods are gaining prominence due to the cost and complexity of gathering real-world data, but they demand a high degree of realism and controllability to be useful. This work introduces two novel methods for synthetic data generation in autonomous driving and medical image analysis, namely MObI and AnydoorMed, respectively. MObI is a first-of-its-kind framework for Multimodal Object Inpainting that leverages a diffusion model to produce realistic and controllable object inpaintings across perceptual modalities, demonstrated simultaneously for camera and lidar. Given a single reference RGB image, MObI enables seamless object insertion into existing multimodal scenes at a specified 3D location, guided by a bounding box, while maintaining semantic consistency and multimodal coherence. Unlike traditional inpainting methods that rely solely on edit masks, this approach uses 3D bounding box conditioning to ensure accurate spatial positioning and realistic scaling. AnydoorMed extends this paradigm to the medical imaging domain, focusing on reference-guided inpainting for mammography scans. It leverages a diffusion-based model to inpaint anomalies with impressive detail preservation, maintaining the reference anomaly's structural integrity while semantically blending it with the surrounding tissue. Together, these methods demonstrate that foundation models for reference-guided inpainting in natural images can be readily adapted to diverse perceptual modalities, paving the way for the next generation of systems capable of constructing highly realistic, controllable and multimodal counterfactual scenarios.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This dissertation introduces two novel methods, MObI and AnydoorMed, for generating realistic and controllable counterfactuals using reference-guided diffusion inpainting. MObI tackles the problem of multimodal object inpainting in autonomous driving scenarios, jointly generating camera and lidar data. AnydoorMed applies a similar approach to medical imaging, focusing on anomaly inpainting in mammography scans. Both methods leverage latent diffusion models and data-efficient adaptation techniques to achieve high realism and semantic coherence, enabling the manipulation and insertion of objects/anomalies while preserving context. The dissertation details the architecture, training procedures, and experimental results of both approaches.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The dissertation presents a contribution, albeit not a groundbreaking one, to the field of generative models, specifically in the context of counterfactual data augmentation.  Here's a breakdown of the assessment:

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Application:** The methods address a real need in both autonomous driving (generating safety-critical scenarios) and medical imaging (augmenting datasets with rare pathologies). This practical focus enhances the paper's potential impact.
    *   **Multimodal Data Generation (MObI):** The joint generation of camera and lidar data is a valuable contribution, as many existing methods focus on a single modality or require complex multi-stage pipelines.  The explicit handling of 3D object conditioning provides a tangible level of control.
    *   **Reference-Guided Approach:** The reference-guided paradigm in AnydoorMed offers more granular control and finer detail capturing in anomalies generations rather than text prompts and coarse masks.
    *   **Adaptation of Existing Techniques:**  Both MOBI and AnydoorMed effectively adapt established techniques like latent diffusion models and reference-guided inpainting to their respective domains. This "building upon" approach allows them to achieve solid performance by standing on the shoulders of giants.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:**  The core technical contributions are *incremental* rather than transformative. They heavily rely on existing foundation models (Stable Diffusion, DINOv2), adapting and fine-tuning them for new tasks. The modifications, while effective, don't fundamentally alter the underlying algorithms.
    *   **Limited Generalization:** The discussion of limitations highlights the challenges in open-world scenarios (MOBI) and the potential for bias and lack of anatomical plausibility (AnydoorMed). These limitations suggest that the methods are relatively sensitive to the training data distribution and may require significant domain-specific engineering to be widely applicable.
    *   **Metric Reliance:** The heavy reliance on metrics like FID and LPIPS can be misleading. While these metrics provide quantitative evaluations, they don't always perfectly correlate with human perception or clinical relevance. The dissertation acknowledges this to some extent.
    *   **Lack of Ground Truth Comparison:** Without robust ground truth comparison, the reliability of the generated scenarios or anomaly is open to discussion.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Potential for Downstream Impact:** The generated data could be valuable for improving the robustness of perception systems in autonomous vehicles and diagnostic accuracy in medical imaging. However, the extent of this impact is contingent on addressing the limitations mentioned above.
    *   **Contribution to the Field:** This will provide valuable guidance for future developments with diffusion models.

**Justification for the Score:**

The score reflects the paper's solid but incremental contribution. The methods are well-executed, address practical problems, and offer a valuable integration of existing techniques. However, the lack of transformative algorithmic novelty and limitations in generalizability prevent it from achieving a higher score. The methods are effectively engineering solutions built on existing foundations rather than groundbreaking theoretical advances.

The significance primarily stems from the potential impact of counterfactual data augmentation in safety-critical domains. However, the concerns about bias, anatomical plausibility, and limited generalization temper this potential, particularly without ground truth comparison.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### FairReason: Balancing Reasoning and Social Bias in MLLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.23067v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenyu Pan, Yutong Zhang, Jianshu Zhang, Haoran Lu, Haozheng Luo, Yuwei Han, Philip S. Yu, Manling Li, Han Liu
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) already achieve state-of-the-art results across a wide range of tasks and modalities. To push their reasoning ability further, recent studies explore advanced prompting schemes and post-training fine-tuning. Although these techniques improve logical accuracy, they frequently leave the models' outputs burdened with pronounced social biases. Clarifying how reasoning gains interact with bias mitigation-and whether the two objectives inherently trade off-therefore remains an open and pressing research problem. Our study begins by benchmarking three bias-mitigation strategies-supervised fine-uning (SFT), knowledge distillation (KD), and rule-based reinforcement learning (RL)-under identical conditions, establishing their baseline strengths and weaknesses. Building on these results, we vary the proportion of debias-focused and reasoning-centric samples within each paradigm to chart the reasoning-versus-bias trade-off. Our sweeps reveal a consistent sweet spot: a roughly 1:4 mix trained with reinforcement learning cuts stereotype scores by 10% while retaining 88% of the model's original reasoning accuracy, offering concrete guidance for balancing fairness and capability in MLLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "FairReason: Balancing Reasoning and Social Bias in MLLMs" addresses the important problem of balancing reasoning ability and social bias mitigation in multimodal large language models (MLLMs).  The authors benchmark three bias-mitigation strategies (SFT, KD, and RL) and then systematically vary the proportion of debias-focused and reasoning-centric samples during training to find a sweet spot that minimizes bias without significantly degrading reasoning performance. The results indicate that a roughly 1:4 mix trained with reinforcement learning can effectively reduce stereotype scores while retaining a large percentage of the original reasoning accuracy. The authors release their best-performing models to facilitate future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in its systematic empirical exploration of the trade-off between reasoning and bias mitigation in MLLMs. While prior work has looked at each objective individually, this paper directly investigates their interaction across different training paradigms and data compositions. Identifying a sweet spot through empirical sweeping is a valuable contribution. The benchmarking of different training strategies specifically for balancing reasoning and bias in MLLMs is relatively novel. However, the individual mitigation strategies (SFT, KD, RL) are well-established.

* **Significance:** The topic is highly relevant. As MLLMs become more powerful, their potential for perpetuating and amplifying social biases is a significant concern. Finding ways to mitigate these biases without sacrificing performance is crucial. The paper's findings provide practical guidance for training more fair and capable MLLMs, especially in resource-constrained settings. The release of best-performing models enhances reproducibility and allows other researchers to build upon this work.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Systematic Approach:** The paper employs a well-designed experimental setup with clear research questions and comprehensive benchmarks.  The controlled variation of data composition (reasoning vs. bias) allows for a thorough understanding of the trade-offs.
    * **Practical Guidance:**  The identification of a "sweet spot" (e.g., 1:4 data mix) provides concrete recommendations for practitioners.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The claims are supported by empirical evidence across multiple model architectures and training paradigms.
    * **Reproducibility:** The authors publicly release their models.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scale:** While the study explores different models, the dataset sizes used for training (5,000 samples) are relatively small, especially for MLLMs. The generalizability of the sweet spot found at this scale to much larger datasets used in production models should be interpreted cautiously.
    * **Benchmark Selection:** While the benchmarks selected are appropriate, there is room for improvement in the bias benchmarks. Some bias benchmarks are known to be problematic. A more comprehensive evaluation across a diverse set of bias benchmarks would strengthen the findings. The bias detection component (evaluating the stereotype score change) can be relatively unsophisticated.
    * **Scope of Bias Mitigation Strategies:** The paper focuses on three specific mitigation strategies. Other promising techniques (e.g., adversarial training, output filtering) are not considered, limiting the scope of the analysis.
    * **Lack of theoretical insight**: While the paper identifies sweet spots, it lacks in-depth theoretical insights into *why* a particular ratio of debiasing data works. More theoretical analysis would be welcome.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the training practices of MLLMs, particularly in situations where computational resources are limited. It provides a starting point for further research on balancing reasoning and fairness in these models.  The open-source release of models will likely encourage adoption and further investigation.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. The paper makes a solid contribution to an important problem. It demonstrates a systematic approach to empirically understanding the trade-off between reasoning and bias mitigation in MLLMs, and provides practical recommendations. However, the limitations related to dataset size, bias benchmark selection, and limited scope of mitigation strategies prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. Further theoretical analysis would be welcome.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Vocabulary-free Fine-grained Visual Recognition via Enriched Contextually Grounded Vision-Language Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.23070v1)
- **Authors**: Dmitry Demidov, Zaigham Zaheer, Omkar Thawakar, Salman Khan, Fahad Shahbaz Khan
- **Abstract**: Fine-grained image classification, the task of distinguishing between visually similar subcategories within a broader category (e.g., bird species, car models, flower types), is a challenging computer vision problem. Traditional approaches rely heavily on fixed vocabularies and closed-set classification paradigms, limiting their scalability and adaptability in real-world settings where novel classes frequently emerge. Recent research has demonstrated that combining large language models (LLMs) with vision-language models (VLMs) makes open-set recognition possible without the need for predefined class labels. However, the existing methods are often limited in harnessing the power of LLMs at the classification phase, and also rely heavily on the guessed class names provided by an LLM without thorough analysis and refinement. To address these bottlenecks, we propose our training-free method, Enriched-FineR (or E-FineR for short), which demonstrates state-of-the-art results in fine-grained visual recognition while also offering greater interpretability, highlighting its strong potential in real-world scenarios and new domains where expert annotations are difficult to obtain. Additionally, we demonstrate the application of our proposed approach to zero-shot and few-shot classification, where it demonstrated performance on par with the existing SOTA while being training-free and not requiring human interventions. Overall, our vocabulary-free framework supports the shift in image classification from rigid label prediction to flexible, language-driven understanding, enabling scalable and generalizable systems for real-world applications. Well-documented code is available on https://github.com/demidovd98/e-finer.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces E-FineR, a novel training-free framework for vocabulary-free fine-grained visual recognition (FGVR). It addresses the limitations of existing methods by enriching vision-language alignment through two key components: (1) automatically generating class-specific contextual descriptions using large language models (LLMs), and (2) employing an advanced class name filtration mechanism that retains multiple semantically plausible labels instead of enforcing rigid top-1 predictions.  E-FineR aims to improve semantic grounding and robustness to ambiguity, crucial for visually similar categories.  It demonstrates state-of-the-art results in vocabulary-free FGVR and comparable performance to zero-shot and few-shot methods without training or manual intervention. The paper emphasizes the shift from rigid label prediction to flexible, language-driven understanding for scalable real-world applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques:
    * **Automated Contextual Description Generation:** While LLMs have been used for prompt generation before, the paper's method of generating *class-specific contextual descriptions* directly from unlabelled images using a VQA-LLM pipeline, tailored for FGVR, represents a valuable contribution. This helps address the limitations of using just class names, especially when visual differences are subtle.
    * **Soft Class Name Filtration:**  The multi-candidate class name filtering, moving beyond strict top-1 selection, is a critical element. It acknowledges the inherent uncertainty in vocabulary-free tasks and avoids prematurely discarding potentially useful labels. This contributes to improved recall.
    * **Fully Automated Pipeline:** The complete training-free and human-intervention-free nature of the framework, allowing it to dynamically construct unsupervised or partially supervised classifiers, is a valuable advantage.

* **Significance:**
    * **Addressing a Practical Problem:** FGVR in real-world scenarios often involves novel classes and limited annotated data. A vocabulary-free approach is a significant step toward addressing this.
    * **Improved Performance:** The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art results in the challenging vocabulary-free setting, which validates the effectiveness of the proposed techniques.
    * **Generalizability:** The results on different datasets (CUB-200, Stanford Cars, Stanford Dogs, Oxford Flowers, and Oxford Pets) suggest good generalization capability.  The comparable performance in zero-shot and few-shot settings further strengthens this claim.
    * **Interpretability:**  Producing semantic class names (rather than just indices) makes the system more interpretable, which is valuable for practical applications.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem and its limitations.
    * **Strong methodology:** The proposed method is well-explained with justifications for design choices.
    * **Comprehensive experimental evaluation:** Thorough comparisons with relevant baselines, ablation studies, and evaluation metrics.
    * **Qualitative results:** Attention map analysis visually supports the claims.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependency on LLMs:** The performance is heavily reliant on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs (ChatGPT and Gemini). Advances or limitations in these models directly affect the E-FineR performance. The paper also doesn't explore the effect of using different LLMs on its performance.
    * **Computational Cost:** LLM inference can be computationally expensive. The paper could benefit from a discussion about the computational cost and efficiency of the approach, especially when considering real-world deployment.
    * **Error Analysis:** A more detailed error analysis, beyond the attention map visualizations, could provide insights into the types of errors the model still makes and potential directions for future improvement. The paper would benefit from identifying the common types of errors.

* **Potential Influence:**
    * **Stimulate Research:** E-FineR could encourage further research into vocabulary-free FGVR, especially focusing on better ways to leverage LLMs for semantic understanding.
    * **Practical Applications:** The framework could be adapted for various real-world applications where annotations are scarce or classes are constantly evolving.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

E-FineR presents a strong contribution by addressing the practical challenge of vocabulary-free FGVR. The combination of automated contextual description generation and soft class name filtration significantly improves performance and interpretability. While the reliance on LLMs and computational cost are valid concerns, the paper demonstrates a solid methodology, comprehensive evaluation, and potential for future research. It provides a well-defined, fully automated, training-free framework that advances the state-of-the-art, which warrants a score of 7.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring In-Context Learning for Frame-Semantic Parsing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.23082v1)
- **Authors**: Diego Garat, Guillermo Moncecchi, Dina Wonsever
- **Abstract**: Frame Semantic Parsing (FSP) entails identifying predicates and labeling their arguments according to Frame Semantics. This paper investigates the use of In-Context Learning (ICL) with Large Language Models (LLMs) to perform FSP without model fine-tuning. We propose a method that automatically generates task-specific prompts for the Frame Identification (FI) and Frame Semantic Role Labeling (FSRL) subtasks, relying solely on the FrameNet database. These prompts, constructed from frame definitions and annotated examples, are used to guide six different LLMs. Experiments are conducted on a subset of frames related to violent events. The method achieves competitive results, with F1 scores of 94.3% for FI and 77.4% for FSRL. The findings suggest that ICL offers a practical and effective alternative to traditional fine-tuning for domain-specific FSP tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of In-Context Learning (ICL) with Large Language Models (LLMs) for Frame Semantic Parsing (FSP). The authors propose a method to automatically generate task-specific prompts for Frame Identification (FI) and Frame Semantic Role Labeling (FSRL) using only the FrameNet database (definitions and annotated examples).  The prompts, including frame definitions and examples, guide the LLMs. The method is evaluated on a subset of frames related to violent events using several LLMs, and achieves competitive results compared to traditional methods, suggesting ICL offers a practical alternative to fine-tuning for domain-specific FSP tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** While ICL for NLP tasks is not entirely novel, applying it specifically to FSP and creating prompts *solely* from FrameNet resources is a contribution.  The automatic prompt generation approach targeting specific FSP subtasks (FI & FSRL) is a differentiating factor. The work is focused on analyzing whether existing pre-trained knowledge can be leveraged effectively.
* **Significance:** The potential significance lies in demonstrating a viable zero-shot or few-shot FSP method that doesn't require extensive fine-tuning. Fine-tuning can be computationally expensive and require substantial labeled data, making ICL a more accessible alternative, especially in situations where labeled data is scarce or domain-specific. The competitive performance compared to previous fine-tuning-based approaches indicates a practical and useful contribution.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Automated Prompt Generation:**  The method to generate prompts from FrameNet is well-defined and replicable.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Evaluation using multiple LLMs allows for a better understanding of the general applicability of the approach.
    * **Clear Problem Formulation:** The division of FSP into FI and FSRL subtasks allows for a more targeted analysis.
    * **Competitive Results:** Achieves competitive results for Frame Identification and Semantic Role Labeling.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is confined to a small subset of FrameNet frames (violent events). Generalizability to the entire FrameNet inventory is not explicitly addressed.
    * **Strict Evaluation Metrics:** While the strict evaluation is understandable, it potentially underestimates the performance by penalizing slight boundary differences. Some discussion of the type of errors produced by the LLMs would be useful.
    * **Lack of Comparison to SOTA Fine-tuned Models:** While the comparison to earlier FSRL approaches is valuable, the paper does not compare against SOTA methods which have been fine-tuned with data augmentation or other strategies.
    * **Limited Ablation Study:** The definition-only prompts also omit examples, and it is unclear if the results are only good with the examples.
* **Potential Influence:** The work demonstrates the feasibility of ICL for a complex semantic task like FSP, which could inspire further research in this direction. This can lead to more efficient FSP systems that are easier to adapt to different domains.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution by exploring ICL for FSP and demonstrating its potential using only FrameNet resources.  The automated prompt generation is a key strength, and the results are promising. However, the limited scope of the evaluation (violent events only), the strict evaluation metric, and the lack of comparison to state-of-the-art fine-tuned models prevent a higher score. While the authors achieve competitive results, the generalizability and superiority over fine-tuned models need further validation. Further, there is little discussion about why the prompts work, and how the LLMs may interpret and leverage the different parts of the prompt.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### On LLM-Assisted Generation of Smart Contracts from Business Processes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.23087v1)
- **Authors**: Fabian Stiehle, Hans Weytjens, Ingo Weber
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have changed the reality of how software is produced. Within the wider software engineering community, among many other purposes, they are explored for code generation use cases from different types of input. In this work, we present an exploratory study to investigate the use of LLMs for generating smart contract code from business process descriptions, an idea that has emerged in recent literature to overcome the limitations of traditional rule-based code generation approaches. However, current LLM-based work evaluates generated code on small samples, relying on manual inspection, or testing whether code compiles but ignoring correct execution. With this work, we introduce an automated evaluation framework and provide empirical data from larger data sets of process models. We test LLMs of different types and sizes in their capabilities of achieving important properties of process execution, including enforcing process flow, resource allocation, and data-based conditions. Our results show that LLM performance falls short of the perfect reliability required for smart contract development. We suggest future work to explore responsible LLM integrations in existing tools for code generation to ensure more reliable output. Our benchmarking framework can serve as a foundation for developing and evaluating such integrations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for generating smart contract code from business process descriptions. The authors introduce an automated benchmarking framework, using the SAP-SAM dataset of process models, to empirically evaluate the performance of various LLMs (both proprietary and open-source) in generating correct Solidity code. The framework assesses properties crucial for blockchain-based process enactment, including enforcing process flow, data-based conditions, and resource allocation. The study reveals that while some LLMs achieve reasonable performance (F1 scores around 0.8), their output remains unreliable and falls short of the required 100% correctness necessary for secure smart contract development on public blockchains. The paper concludes that LLMs are not currently suitable for direct code generation but suggests future directions for responsible LLM integration, such as using them for code suggestion, verification, and extending existing code generation tools rather than replacing them.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the automated evaluation framework for assessing LLMs in smart contract code generation from business process models. While some prior works exist in this area, they often rely on manual inspection, small sample sizes, or focus primarily on syntactic correctness. The framework's open availability is also a positive factor, promoting reproducibility and further research. The specific application of the SAP-SAM dataset is another unique aspect. However, the core idea of using LLMs for code generation is not new, and the paper's findings largely confirm existing concerns about LLM reliability in high-stakes domains.

*   **Significance:** The paper makes a significant contribution by empirically demonstrating the limitations of current LLMs for smart contract generation in a relatively rigorous and automated fashion. The benchmarking framework provides a valuable tool for future research in this area, and the paper's findings help to temper overly optimistic expectations about LLM capabilities in blockchain development. The paper's discussion of alternative integration strategies (e.g., using LLMs for verification) is also a valuable contribution. A weakness is the lack of exploration regarding improving the prompts to elicit better LLM results.
    The reliance on BPMN choreographies as the model of choice is another factor that limits the paper's significance, as the adoption of this model is still not widespread.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined research question.
    *   Automated and reproducible evaluation framework.
    *   Use of a large and diverse dataset (SAP-SAM).
    *   Empirical validation of LLM performance.
    *   Discussion of alternative integration strategies.
    *   Open availability of code and data.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The findings are largely confirmatory of existing concerns.
    *   Limited exploration of prompt engineering strategies for improving LLM performance.
    *   Relatively narrow focus on BPMN choreographies.
    *   The reliance on models being "up" and available through the OpenRouter platform affects the reliability of repeating the experiments.
    *   No real exploration of integrating with existing tools, limiting its practical impact.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's main influence is likely to be in guiding future research efforts towards more responsible and effective LLM integration strategies in smart contract development. The benchmarking framework could become a standard tool for evaluating new LLM-based code generation approaches. The open availability of the data and code should facilitate further experimentation and refinement of the framework.
**Justification for the Score:**

The paper offers a valuable, but incremental contribution to the field. It provides a well-engineered benchmarking framework and empirically supports the existing concerns about LLM reliability, while also charting a course for more effective integration. The weaknesses, however, limit its overall impact. The novelty is reasonably high, but the confirmatory nature of the results and limitations in addressing the problem constrain the paper's potential influence.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Beyond Rigid AI: Towards Natural Human-Machine Symbiosis for Interoperative Surgical Assistance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.23088v1)
- **Authors**: Lalithkumar Seenivasan, Jiru Xu, Roger D. Soberanis Mukul, Hao Ding, Grayson Byrd, Yu-Chun Ku, Jose L. Porras, Masaru Ishii, Mathias Unberath
- **Abstract**: Emerging surgical data science and robotics solutions, especially those designed to provide assistance in situ, require natural human-machine interfaces to fully unlock their potential in providing adaptive and intuitive aid. Contemporary AI-driven solutions remain inherently rigid, offering limited flexibility and restricting natural human-machine interaction in dynamic surgical environments. These solutions rely heavily on extensive task-specific pre-training, fixed object categories, and explicit manual-prompting. This work introduces a novel Perception Agent that leverages speech-integrated prompt-engineered large language models (LLMs), segment anything model (SAM), and any-point tracking foundation models to enable a more natural human-machine interaction in real-time intraoperative surgical assistance. Incorporating a memory repository and two novel mechanisms for segmenting unseen elements, Perception Agent offers the flexibility to segment both known and unseen elements in the surgical scene through intuitive interaction. Incorporating the ability to memorize novel elements for use in future surgeries, this work takes a marked step towards human-machine symbiosis in surgical procedures. Through quantitative analysis on a public dataset, we show that the performance of our agent is on par with considerably more labor-intensive manual-prompting strategies. Qualitatively, we show the flexibility of our agent in segmenting novel elements (instruments, phantom grafts, and gauze) in a custom-curated dataset. By offering natural human-machine interaction and overcoming rigidity, our Perception Agent potentially brings AI-based real-time assistance in dynamic surgical environments closer to reality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Perception Agent," a novel AI-driven system designed to provide natural human-machine interaction for real-time intraoperative surgical assistance. The system leverages speech-integrated large language models (LLMs), the Segment Anything Model (SAM), and any-point tracking foundation models to segment both known and unseen surgical elements. Perception Agent incorporates a memory repository and two novel mechanisms – object-centric and reference-based segmentation – for handling previously unseen elements.  The system aims to overcome the rigidity of current AI solutions in surgical environments by allowing intuitive interaction and memorization of novel elements for future use, moving towards human-machine symbiosis in surgical procedures.  The paper presents quantitative and qualitative evaluations demonstrating the agent's performance on public and custom-curated datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its integration of several existing AI components (LLMs, SAM, point tracking) into a cohesive system designed specifically for flexible surgical scene understanding.  The main contributions are the object-centric and reference-based segmentation mechanisms, which extend SAM's capabilities to handle novel elements without requiring extensive retraining or manual prompting.  The integration of speech for natural language interaction also adds to the novelty.  However, individually, none of the underlying models are novel. The innovation is in their specific configuration and application to the surgical domain, along with the memory mechanism for improved performance over time.
*   **Significance:** The work addresses a significant limitation of current AI-driven surgical assistance systems: their rigidity and inability to adapt to dynamic surgical environments or handle previously unseen elements.  By enabling natural human-machine interaction and memorization of novel elements, Perception Agent has the potential to enhance surgical workflows, improve decision-making, and ultimately lead to better patient outcomes.  The ability to segment and track custom grafts or surgical sponges, for example, could directly impact surgical precision and reduce complications. The modular design and reliance on pre-trained foundation models also increases the potential for adoption and integration into existing surgical systems, and offers a means for annotation of new surgical images and videos to improve future foundation models.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical focus:** The paper directly addresses a real-world problem in surgical assistance.
    *   **Modular design:**  The use of pre-trained foundation models and a modular architecture makes the system adaptable and extensible.
    *   **Clear evaluation:** The paper includes both quantitative (on a public dataset) and qualitative (on a custom dataset) evaluations to demonstrate the system's performance and flexibility.
    *   **Addresses rigidity:** The core contribution of object and reference based segmentation for novel items overcomes a significant problem in surgical AI.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental advance:** The work primarily integrates existing components rather than introducing fundamentally new algorithms or models.
    *   **Limited quantitative evaluation:** Quantitative results for novel segmentation approaches would better validate the new mechanisms of the system. Quantitative evaluation is mostly on previously known objects in a public dataset.
    *   **Limited scope:** The paper focuses primarily on segmentation and lacks broader integration with surgical robots or planning/decision-making tools.
    *   **Simulated environment:** The simulated surgical environment used for qualitative evaluation may not fully capture the complexities of real-world surgery.
    *   **Limited discussion of errors:** The paper doesn't discuss failure cases of the perception agent, and there is no discussion of the limitations.

* **Long Term Significance:** The memory mechanism and the flexibility to segment novel items moves AI driven surgical assistance one step closer to autonomous assistance and learning in the surgical environment. This is an important step towards more useful surgical AI.
* **Limitations of Approach:** The use of speech, especially casual speech, can introduce errors in understanding. While it allows the agent to interact with the surgical team in a natural way, there may be misunderstandings and difficulties interpreting the commands. Also, the current study is limited by being conducted on simulated surgeries.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to surgical AI by addressing the critical issue of rigidity in current systems and introducing a more flexible and adaptable architecture. However, the incremental nature of the advance (integrating existing components) and the limited quantitative evaluation of the novel segmentation mechanisms prevent a higher score. The work has significant potential to impact surgical workflows and improve patient outcomes, making it a worthwhile contribution despite its limitations. The modular approach and memory retention allow the potential for long term performance enhancement and greater application across varied surgical situations.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### On the Sustainability of AI Inferences in the Edge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.23093v1)
- **Authors**: Ghazal Sobhani, Md. Monzurul Amin Ifath, Tushar Sharma, Israat Haque
- **Abstract**: The proliferation of the Internet of Things (IoT) and its cutting-edge AI-enabled applications (e.g., autonomous vehicles and smart industries) combine two paradigms: data-driven systems and their deployment on the edge. Usually, edge devices perform inferences to support latency-critical applications. In addition to the performance of these resource-constrained edge devices, their energy usage is a critical factor in adopting and deploying edge applications. Examples of such devices include Raspberry Pi (RPi), Intel Neural Compute Stick (INCS), NVIDIA Jetson nano (NJn), and Google Coral USB (GCU). Despite their adoption in edge deployment for AI inferences, there is no study on their performance and energy usage for informed decision-making on the device and model selection to meet the demands of applications. This study fills the gap by rigorously characterizing the performance of traditional, neural networks, and large language models on the above-edge devices. Specifically, we analyze trade-offs among model F1 score, inference time, inference power, and memory usage. Hardware and framework optimization, along with external parameter tuning of AI models, can balance between model performance and resource usage to realize practical edge AI deployments.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "On the Sustainability of AI Inferences in the Edge" following the instructions.

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the critical issue of energy efficiency and sustainability in edge AI deployments. It investigates the performance and resource trade-offs across various AI models (traditional ML, DL, LLMs) and hardware targets (Raspberry Pi, Intel Neural Compute Stick, NVIDIA Jetson Nano, Google Coral USB Accelerator). The authors propose a unified measurement approach combining hardware-based power profiling and software-based performance monitoring, aiming for consistent evaluation across platforms. The paper provides detailed guidelines for executing models on edge devices, highlighting best practices for optimization. The results demonstrate the impact of domain-specific hardware and software designs, the trade-offs between performance and resource usage, and the effects of parameter tuning on energy consumption. The study aims to guide users in selecting the right combination of AI models, edge devices, frameworks, and parameters for successful and sustainable edge AI deployments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's strength lies in its comprehensive characterization of performance and resource usage across a diverse set of edge devices, AI models, and frameworks. While prior work has examined specific models or hardware, this paper offers a more holistic view, including LLMs, which is a timely addition. The development of a unified measurement scheme is also a valuable contribution, addressing the fragmentation in existing evaluation methodologies. However, some aspects of the work build on established techniques for model optimization and hardware evaluation. The study, therefore, presents incremental rather than radical novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a highly relevant and important problem: the sustainability of AI inferences at the edge. With the increasing deployment of AI-enabled IoT devices, energy consumption becomes a pressing concern. The insights provided by this study can help developers and organizations make informed decisions about model selection, hardware configuration, and optimization strategies to minimize energy footprint without compromising performance. The systematic evaluation across multiple models, frameworks, and edge platforms makes this work practically useful. However, the real-world impact hinges on the actual adoption of the proposed measurement scheme and guidelines by practitioners.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive evaluation across various models, hardware, and frameworks.
    *   Unified measurement approach promoting consistent and reproducible evaluation.
    *   Detailed guidelines for model execution and optimization on edge devices.
    *   Inclusion of LLMs in the evaluation, addressing an emerging trend in edge AI.
    *   Publicly available scripts and data for reproducibility.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The novelty is incremental rather than revolutionary.
    *   The impact is dependent on the adoption of the proposed methodology by practitioners.
    *   Some of the optimization techniques are well-established, although their systematic application in this context is valuable.
    *   The specific performance results are tied to the specific hardware and software versions used, which may become outdated.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the field by providing a benchmark for evaluating edge AI platforms and models. The unified measurement scheme can serve as a foundation for future research and development in energy-efficient edge AI. The guidelines can help practitioners make informed decisions and optimize their deployments for sustainability.
*   **Areas for Improvement:** Future work could explore:

    *   Extend the measurement scheme to include other edge AI hardware (e.g., newer Jetson boards, specialized AI accelerators).
    *   Investigate automated parameter tuning techniques in more detail.
    *   Assess the impact of various deployment strategies (e.g., containerization) on energy consumption.
    *   Develop a dynamic resource allocation framework that adapts to fluctuating workload demands to save energy.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by providing a comprehensive and systematic evaluation of edge AI platforms and models from a sustainability perspective. While the novelty is incremental, the significance of the problem and the practical utility of the results justify a solid score.

### ChatVis: Large Language Model Agent for Generating Scientific Visualizations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.23096v1)
- **Authors**: Tom Peterka, Tanwi Mallick, Orcun Yildiz, David Lenz, Cory Quammen, Berk Geveci
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are rapidly increasing in capability, but they still struggle with highly specialized programming tasks such as scientific visualization. We present an LLM assistant, ChatVis, that aids the LLM to generate Python code for ParaView scientific visualization tasks, without the need for retraining or fine-tuning the LLM. ChatVis employs chain-of-thought prompt simplification, retrieval-augmented prompt generation using a vector database of documentation and code examples, and error checking with iterative prompt feedback to correct errors until a visualization is produced. An integral part of our approach is a benchmark suite of canonical visualization tasks, ParaView regression tests, and scientific use cases that includes comprehensive evaluation metrics. We evaluate our visualization assistant by comparing results with a variety of top-performing unassisted LLMs. We find that all the metrics are significantly improved with ChatVis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents ChatVis, an LLM assistant designed to improve the generation of scientific visualizations using the ParaView software. ChatVis uses Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) by leveraging a vector database of ParaView documentation and code examples. The system also includes an iterative error correction loop to refine the generated Python scripts until they execute successfully. The paper evaluates ChatVis against several state-of-the-art LLMs on a custom benchmark suite comprising canonical visualization tasks, ParaView regression tests, and scientific use cases, showing ChatVis significantly outperforms the unassisted models in terms of syntax correctness and image quality. The paper also compares the use of RAG versus few-shot prompting.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic approach to combining several techniques (RAG, chain-of-thought prompting, and iterative error correction) to address the specific challenges of generating scientific visualizations with LLMs. While individual techniques like RAG and iterative refinement are not new, the integration and application within the context of scientific visualization is a valuable contribution. The benchmark suite of scientific visualization tasks is a solid contribution that enhances the reproducibility and rigor of the study.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its potential to make scientific visualization more accessible to domain scientists who may not have extensive programming expertise. By automating the generation of ParaView scripts, ChatVis can streamline the visualization workflow and enable scientists to more easily explore and analyze their data. This reduces the reliance on visualization experts and accelerates the discovery process. The work directly addresses a known limitation of current LLMs in their ability to handle specialized programming tasks. The benchmark is a valuable contribution to the field that will help advance LLM use within scientific visualization.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper provides a thorough evaluation of ChatVis using a diverse benchmark suite and multiple evaluation metrics, including syntax correctness and image quality.
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The combination of RAG, chain-of-thought, and iterative correction is a well-defined and effective strategy for improving LLM performance.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The focus on ParaView, a widely used scientific visualization tool, enhances the practical relevance of the work.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The documentation of the benchmark suite and the intention to release ChatVis as open-source software improve the reproducibility and accessibility of the research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** While ParaView is a popular tool, the system is specifically tailored for it. Generalizing the approach to other visualization tools (e.g., VisIt) might require significant effort.
    *   **Reliance on GPT-4o:** The paper relies on a specific LLM, GPT-4o, and the results may vary with different models. While the framework is the main contribution, the performance differences with other models should be discussed more extensively, particularly since LLMs are constantly evolving.
    *   **Image Comparison Metrics:** The reliance on image comparison metrics (SSIM, PSNR, LPIPS) might not fully capture the nuances of scientific visualizations, particularly when dealing with complex data or subtle features. However, the paper acknowledges this limitation and justifies the choice of metrics used.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:**  While the paper analyzes two styles of prompts, a greater effort should be placed on characterizing the impact of different prompt strategies and techniques, particularly with respect to prompt engineering, and the robustness of the system to varying prompt quality.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more accessible and automated scientific visualization tools.  It demonstrates the feasibility of using LLMs to assist in complex programming tasks and provides a valuable framework for future research in this area. The benchmark suite can serve as a standard for evaluating the performance of other visualization assistants.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by demonstrating a practical approach to leveraging LLMs for scientific visualization. The system provides a good solution, while showing good documentation and reproducibility. While the approach relies on specific tools (ParaView) and its implementation may be limited, the system has good integration of RAG, error correction, and chain of thought prompting. The framework and evaluation methodology are solid, and the demonstrated results are compelling. The weaknesses are somewhat minor and do not overshadow the overall value of the work.
